People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.9, F06.81\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Your Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’m having trouble keeping things organized at work. Tickets, receipts, notes, customer jobs… they all end up piled together, and then I don’t know what to deal with first.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay organized or focused in other areas of your life, like at home or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s at work. I mean… that’s where I really notice it. The tickets, receipts, notes from the workshop, customer jobs—everything ends up in one pile, and then I just kind of freeze because I don’t know what to handle first.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you find it difficult to stay organized or keep track of tasks, like managing household chores, personal items, or following through on plans?\n\nPatient: I guess outside of work I don’t notice it as much. It’s really the work stuff… the tickets and receipts and notes, customer jobs, they all get mixed together, and then I can’t tell what I’m supposed to handle first.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself struggling to keep track of things or feeling overwhelmed even when you're not at work, like when you're at home or running errands?\n\nPatient: Not really that I can say. It’s mostly the work stuff… the tickets and receipts and notes from the workshop, customer jobs. They all end up together, and then I don’t know what I’m supposed to do first.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or worried about things getting out of control, even when you're not dealing with the tickets or receipts at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know… I don’t think about it that way. I mostly notice it when the work stuff piles up. Outside of that, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: When you're not at work, do you ever feel anxious or worried about things in general, even when there's no pile of tickets or receipts to deal with?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I feel worried in general. I haven’t really noticed that, unless there’s something specific going on.\n\nDoctor: When you're completely away from work, like on your days off, do you ever feel anxious or worried about things even when there's nothing specific to deal with?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, on days off I don’t usually sit around worrying if there’s nothing going on. I might have things to do, but I wouldn’t call it anxious, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home or in other places outside of work, do you have trouble organizing tasks or keeping track of what you need to do, even when there are things you know you should be handling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess some. It’s more obvious at work, though. The tickets and receipts and notes all end up mixed together, and then I don’t know what to do first. At home I don’t think it’s as bad, or maybe I just don’t notice it the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or worried even when there’s no specific problem or task to deal with?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not if there’s really nothing going on. I mean… I haven’t noticed that kind of worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or worried when there’s absolutely nothing on your plate—no tasks, no work, no specific problems to solve?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. If there’s really nothing I have to deal with, I’m usually not sitting there worrying.\n\nDoctor: Even if there’s nothing specific happening, do you ever feel a general sense of worry or anxiety just sitting with your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, if nothing’s going on, I don’t usually feel worried just sitting there. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
